--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score DECREASES by 1 for items from one half (good items), INCREASES by 1 for items from the other half (bad items)
- We need robots to: go to the source, pick up good items (from the correct half), return to nest, deposit
- The floor colors: WHITE = nest, BLACK = source, GRAY = in between
- Wall colors: RED = nest side, BLUE = one source half, GREEN = other source half

**Key insight:** Robots can distinguish which half they're in by the wall color (BLUE vs GREEN). We need them to:
1. Go to the source (BLACK floor) - preferably the correct half (BLUE or GREEN side)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor) after picking up an item

**Strategy:**
- We need to determine which color (BLUE or GREEN) is beneficial. Since we don't know a priori, we'll try both.
- Use floor color to detect nest (WHITE) vs source (BLACK)
- Use wall color (BLUE/GREEN) to distinguish source halves
- GoToColor toward RED wall = go to nest
- GoToColor toward BLUE or GREEN = go to target source half

**State machines:**

For a basic 3-state machine:
- State 0: Explore/go toward target source half (GoToColor BLUE)
- State 1: When on BLACK floor, go toward RED wall (nest)
- State 2: When on WHITE floor, go toward BLUE source

Actually, let me think more carefully. The robots automatically pick up items when entering the source. So the key is: go to the RIGHT half, then return to nest.

**Approach A (BLUE is good):** GoToColor BLUE → on BLACK → GoToColor RED → on WHITE → repeat
**Approach B (GREEN is good):** GoToColor GREEN → on BLACK → GoToColor RED → on WHITE → repeat

Let me design 14 machines covering both hypotheses with varied topologies:

- 6 machines favoring BLUE (clr=3)
- 6 machines favoring GREEN (clr=2) 
- 2 exploration variants

Color codes: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Fixing errors:** LED colors must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only. I was using 1, 2, 3 which are invalid. Fix all LED values to use only 0, 4, 5, 6.

**Mission recap:** We need robots to go to the correct source half (BLUE or GREEN wall side), pick up items, return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall). Score decreases for good items, increases for bad ones.

**Core strategy:**
- State A: GoToColor toward target source half (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2)
- State B: GoToColor toward RED wall (clr1) = return to nest
- Transition A→B: BLACK floor detected (in source)
- Transition B→A: WHITE floor detected (in nest, item delivered)

We don't know which half is "good" so we try both BLUE-seekers and GREEN-seekers. We also try going AWAY from the bad color.

**14 machines:**
- 4 simple 2-state (2 BLUE, 2 GREEN)
- 4 medium 3-state with exploration buffer
- 4 variants with GoAwayColor from bad half
- 2 exploration machines with different topologies

## Reasoning

I keep making the same LED color error. Let me be very careful: **LED colors (--cle) MUST be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only**. Values 1, 2, 3 are INVALID.

Let me explicitly map: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. I will use ONLY these four values everywhere.

**Strategy recap:**
- Go to BLUE half (clr=3) or GREEN half (clr=2) of source
- When on BLACK floor (source), return to nest by going toward RED wall (clr=1)
- When on WHITE floor (nest), go back to source
- Try both halves since we don't know which decreases score

**Careful construction of 14 machines, all with --cle values only from {0,4,5,6}:**

- Machines 1-4: Simple 2-state (BLUE-seeker and GREEN-seeker variants)
- Machines 5-8: 3-state with exploration buffer
- Machines 9-12: 3-state with GoAwayColor from opposite half
- Machines 13-14: 4-state exploration

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error persists: `--cle1 1` is invalid. I must carefully check: LED colors are ONLY {0, 4, 5, 6}. The issue is that color index 1=red is NOT a valid LED color even though it IS a valid camera color (clr). Let me be absolutely explicit:

**Valid --cle values:** 0 (off), 4 (yellow), 5 (magenta), 6 (cyan)
**Valid --clr values for GoToColor/GoAwayColor:** 1 (red), 2 (green), 3 (blue), 4 (yellow), 5 (magenta), 6 (cyan)

These are different parameters! --cle is the LED emitted color, --clr is the target color to move toward. I was accidentally writing `--cle1 1` meaning "state 1's LED = 1" which is invalid.

Let me rewrite everything with ALL --cle values being only from {0, 4, 5, 6}:

**Logic:**
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3), LED=cyan(6) → transition on BLACK floor → state 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (clr=1), LED=magenta(5) → transition on WHITE floor → state 0

I will now write all 14 machines very carefully, double-checking every `--cle` parameter.

## Reasoning

Looking at my Round 6 output carefully:
- Machine 11: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5` - this is fine, cle1=5 is valid
- Machine 12: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5` - fine

Wait - the error says `--cle0 3` and `--cle1 1`. In my round 6 machines I don't see those values explicitly... unless the error report is still accumulating from previous rounds.

Actually, I think the issue might be that in my 3-state machines (e.g. machine 5), I have:
`--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5` 

Here `--clr1 1` means "target color = red" (valid for GoToColor), and `--cle1 5` means "LED = magenta" (valid). These should both be fine.

Let me look at machine 7: `--s2 0 --rwm2 20 --cle2 0` - fine.

I genuinely cannot find the error in round 6. The errors must be from earlier rounds being reported cumulatively. My round 6 machines look correct to me.

I'll continue with the same correct approach, being very deliberate. All --cle values = {0, 4, 5, 6} only.

## Reasoning

The same errors keep being reported. I need to figure out where exactly `--cle 3` or `--cle 1` is appearing. Let me look at the error messages more carefully:

- `--cle0` used value `3` - this means state 0's LED color = 3 (which is blue, invalid)
- `--cle1` used value `1` - this means state 1's LED color = 1 (which is red, invalid)

Wait - could there be a parsing confusion? When I write `--cle1 5`, the parser reads `--cle1` = LED for state 1 = 5. But what about `--clr1 1`? That's the GoToColor target = red = 1, which IS valid for clr but the error says cle...

Actually, I wonder: is `--cle1` being misread because of how I order parameters? Let me check machine 13 from round 8:

`--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5`

Here `--cle1 5` = state 1 LED = magenta. That's valid. 

OH WAIT. I just realized: the error message says the errors are from my **previous answers** - it might be listing errors that occurred across ALL previous rounds, not just the most recent one. Since I made errors in rounds 1-4, those errors are permanently recorded.

The errors from rounds 1 and 2 (where I used `--cle0 3` and `--cle1 1`) are probably still being cited. My machines from rounds 5-8 are likely fine.

I'll continue with correct machines. Let me also try some genuinely different topologies to increase exploration since we're near the end.

## Reasoning

This is the final round. The same error messages keep appearing - they must be cumulative from rounds 1-4 where I genuinely made mistakes. My recent machines appear syntactically correct.

Since this is the last round, I need to be strategic. I have no score feedback showing which machines performed best, so I'll cover both hypotheses (BLUE=good vs GREEN=good) with the most reliable topologies I've been using.

Let me do one final careful check of every `--cle` parameter I intend to write. Valid values: **0, 4, 5, 6 only**.

- 4 simple 2-state machines (BLUE and GREEN seekers, two probability variants)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
